Decorrelation transition in the Wigner minor process
Authors: Zhigang Bao, Giorgio Cipolloni, László Erdős, Joscha Henheik, Oleksii Kolupaiev
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We consider the Wigner minor process, i.e. the eigenvalues of an N× NN×N Wigner matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) together with the eigenvalues of all its n× nn×n minors, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n)}$$\end{document}H(n), n≤ Nn≤N. The top eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and those of its immediate minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-1)}$$\end{document}H(N-1) are very strongly correlated, but this correlation becomes weaker for smaller minors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) as k increases. For the GUE minor process the critical transition regime around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3 was analyzed by Forrester and Nagao (Forrester and Nagao, J. Stat. Mech. (08): P08011, 2011) providing an explicit formula for the nontrivial joint correlation function. We prove that this formula is universal, i.e. it holds for the Wigner minor process. Moreover, we give a complete analysis of the sub- and supercritical regimes both for eigenvalues and for the corresponding eigenvector overlaps, thus we prove the decorrelation transition in full generality.

Introduction

In the past fifteen years, research on the universality problem of random matrix spectral statistics has seen significant progress. Although most of the work focuses on the limiting properties of spectral statistics of a single random matrix, some understanding has also been gained about the relationship between spectral statistics of two dependent/coupled matrices. This includes, e.g., [61] for the dynamical correlation of Dyson Brownian Motion (DBM), [28] for the study of weakly correlated DBMs, [12] for the eigenvector coupling/decoupling of random matrices under resampling, and [23, 25] for the asymptotic independence of spectrally localized resolvents between two sufficiently distant deformations of the same random matrix. In the present work, we study a model that has a very refined understanding under the Gaussian setting but much less is known under the general setting. This is the Wigner minor process, which we define below.

Model and results

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H\equiv H^{(N)}=(h_{ij})_{N,N}$$\end{document}H≡H(N)=(hij)N,N be a N-dimensional Wigner matrix, i.e. a random real symmetric or complex Hermitian matrix with independent centered and entries of with variance 1/N, up to the symmetry constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{ij}=\overline{h_{ji}}$$\end{document}hij=hji¯. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(n)}\ge \ldots \ge \lambda _n^{(n)}$$\end{document}λ1(n)≥…≥λn(n) are the ordered eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} Hereafter the multi-level particle system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(n, \lambda _{i}^{(n)}): i\in [ n], n\in [ N] \}\in [ N] \times \textbf{R}$$\end{document}{(n,λi(n)):i∈[n],n∈[N]}∈[N]×R will be called the Wigner minor process, where we used the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[ m]=\{1, \ldots , m\}$$\end{document}[m]={1,…,m}. There is a point at (n, λ )(n,λ) if and only if the minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n)}$$\end{document}H(n) has an eigenvalue λ λ; cf. The rescaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\lambda }^{(N-k)}_j:=(1-k/N)^{-1/2}\lambda ^{(N-k)}_j$$\end{document}λ~j(N-k):=(1-k/N)-1/2λj(N-k) ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\lambda }^{(N-k)}_1$$\end{document}λ~1(N-k) remain close to 2 for all k. In contrast, in the supercritical regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3) the eigenvalue pattern deviates significantly from the subcritical case, consistent with the asymptotic independence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k)

The rescaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\lambda }^{(N-k)}_j:=(1-k/N)^{-1/2}\lambda ^{(N-k)}_j$$\end{document}λ~j(N-k):=(1-k/N)-1/2λj(N-k) ensures that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\lambda }^{(N-k)}_1$$\end{document}λ~1(N-k) remain close to 2 for all k. In contrast, in the supercritical regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3) the eigenvalue pattern deviates significantly from the subcritical case, consistent with the asymptotic independence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k)

When focusing on a single level, i.e., the eigenvalues of a single \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N), there is a vast amount of literature about the universality of their local statistics. Among various results, the edge universality, also known as the Tracy-Widom law for the extreme eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) has been established under rather general condition [4, 15, 55, 68], even with an explicit rate of convergence [66, 67]. However, beyond integrable models such as GUE, very little is known when we extend the study from a single-level system to a multi-level system, when correlations of eigenvalues from different levels become relevant.

In this paper, we establish a phase transition of the joint distribution of the top eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(n)}$$\end{document}λ1(n), and of the overlap of their corresponding top eigenvectors.1 From now on, we fix k = k(N) := N-nk=k(N):=N-n and consider the following three regimes: (i)In the supercritical regime (see Theorem 2.2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are asymptotically independent, i.e. both follow the Tracy-Widom law after suitable normalization, but independently. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost orthogonal, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \big | \ll 1$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|≪1.(ii)In the critical regime (see Theorem 2.3), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, we prove that the joint correlation function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) is asymptotically given by a non-trivial universal function. In the complex Hermitian case, this function has been computed by Forrester and Nagao for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE) [41].(iii)In the subcritical regime (see Theorem 2.4), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, we prove that the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ1(N-k) is an asymptotically Gaussian random variable with variance of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}/N$$\end{document}k/N, i.e. much smaller than their individual fluctuation scales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}N-2/3. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are strongly correlated. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost aligned, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \approx 1$$\end{document}〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉≈1.We call this phase transition the decorrelation transition, inspired by the discovery of the critical level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3 by Forrester and Nagao [41] for the GUE minor process; see Sect. 1.2 below.

(i)In the supercritical regime (see Theorem 2.2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are asymptotically independent, i.e. both follow the Tracy-Widom law after suitable normalization, but independently. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost orthogonal, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \big | \ll 1$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|≪1.

In the supercritical regime (see Theorem 2.2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are asymptotically independent, i.e. both follow the Tracy-Widom law after suitable normalization, but independently. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost orthogonal, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \big | \ll 1$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|≪1.

(ii)In the critical regime (see Theorem 2.3), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, we prove that the joint correlation function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) is asymptotically given by a non-trivial universal function. In the complex Hermitian case, this function has been computed by Forrester and Nagao for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE) [41].

In the critical regime (see Theorem 2.3), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, we prove that the joint correlation function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) is asymptotically given by a non-trivial universal function. In the complex Hermitian case, this function has been computed by Forrester and Nagao for the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE) [41].

(iii)In the subcritical regime (see Theorem 2.4), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, we prove that the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ1(N-k) is an asymptotically Gaussian random variable with variance of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}/N$$\end{document}k/N, i.e. much smaller than their individual fluctuation scales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}N-2/3. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are strongly correlated. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost aligned, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \approx 1$$\end{document}〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉≈1.

In the subcritical regime (see Theorem 2.4), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, we prove that the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ1(N-k) is an asymptotically Gaussian random variable with variance of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}/N$$\end{document}k/N, i.e. much smaller than their individual fluctuation scales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}N-2/3. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) are strongly correlated. Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost aligned, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle \approx 1$$\end{document}〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉≈1.

Related works

We now discuss several related problems studied in the literature.

The GUE minor process

Similarly to the single-level case, historical studies start from the complex Gaussian case, i.e., GUE, due to its accessible determinantal nature. The GUE minor process is first studied by Baryshnikov in [8], where it is shown that the largest eigenvalues of the GUE minors have the same law as the Brownian last passage percolation. It is then proved in [50] that the GUE minor process is determinantal, and the authors established a connection with the random tilings of the Aztec diamond. We also refer to [43] for a connection between the six vertex model and the GUE minor process. The GUE minor process with external sources is considered in [2]. The scaling limit of the local statistics of GUE minor process and their extensions to other invariant ensembles have been studied in [21, 41, 44, 45, 59, 62], in both edge and bulk regimes. We also refer to [1, 40, 44, 45, 73] for a study of GUE minor process from a dynamical point of view. In addition, recent studies on the covariance estimates and universality of eigengaps between two successive levels of GUE minor process in the bulk regime turn out to be the key technical inputs for the analysis of random hives; see [63, 64, 69]. In the edge regime, the scaling limit of GUE minor process with external sources plays a fundamental role in the distribution of the eigenvectors of spiked GUE in the critical regime of BBP transition; see [7].

Extending the result from Gaussian model to the generally distributed model and proving the universality for Wigner minor process in various local regimes, is first done in Huang’s recent paper [48]. Especially, in [48], the universality of the minor process is proved in the edge regime, when the ranks of the minors differ by a fixed number. Roughly speaking, in this case, up to the first order, the fluctuation of the leading eigenvalues of level n synchronizes with that of level N when n=N-kn=N-k with a fixed k, which is on the scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-\frac{2}{3}}$$\end{document}N-23 and given asymptotically by the Tracy-Widom law. The difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ1(N-k) fluctuates on a much smaller scale (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim N^{-1}$$\end{document}∼N-1), asymptotically given by Gamma distribution, which has been previously observed for the GUE minor process [45]. It is also shown in [48] that the local scaling limit for the Wigner minor process is universally given by the the bead process for general \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Sine}_\beta $$\end{document}Sineβ process in the bulk, as initially constructed for the Hermite β β ensemble in [62]. We also refer to [42] for the edge behavior of so-called general Gaussian β β corner processes.

Noise sensitivity

Another related problem concerns the noise sensitivity of the eigenvectors of random matrices. In [12], the authors study what will happen to the top eigenvector of a Wigner matrix if K randomly chosen entries of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) are resampled (see also [11] for sparse matrices). More specifically, they explore the relationship between the top eigenvector of the original Wigner matrix and the one after resampling and identify a threshold of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\sim N^{5/3}$$\end{document}K∼N5/3. In particular, the following phase transition is observed in [12]: (i) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\ll N^{5/3}$$\end{document}K≪N5/3, the two top eigenvectors are almost colinear; (ii) If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\gg N^{5/3}$$\end{document}K≫N5/3, the two top eigenvectors are almost orthogonal. We note that the threshold here matches that found by Forrester and Nagao for the minor process, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{2/3}$$\end{document}N2/3 columns/rows consist of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{5/3}$$\end{document}N5/3 entries. A first-order estimate on the difference between the largest eigenvalues of two matrices before and after the resampling is also provided in [12]. However, the much more delicate relation between the two top eigenvalues at the fluctuation level remain unexplored for this model. We expect that our approach developed for the minor process can be adapted to the resampling model as well.

Law of the fractional logarithm

As the last related problem, we mention a version of the law of iterated logarithm for the largest eigenvalue of the Wigner matrix. More precisely, in [65] and [9], a law of fractional logarithm (LFL) for the largest eigenvalue of GUE is established, where a key ingredient is the asymptotic coupling and decoupling of the tail events of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(n)}$$\end{document}λ1(n) in the sub- and supercritical regimes, respectively. In the decorrelation transition studied in this paper, we consider the relationship between the two largest eigenvalues on their typical fluctuation scale. In a separate paper [6], we extend these relations to the small/moderate deviation regime and prove LFL for the largest eigenvalue of a generally distributed Wigner matrix.

Ideas of the proofs

We now describe our proof strategy used to prove the decorrelation transition. Each of the three regimes (subcritical, critical, and supercritical) uses quite different techniques; for this reason, we now explain the approach in each regime separately.

In the subcritical regime (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3), using perturbation theory, we employ a recursion formula which gives an expression of the top eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n)}$$\end{document}H(n) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-n \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}N-n≪N2/3 in terms of averages of its eigenvectors and eigenvalues of its minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n-1)}$$\end{document}H(n-1) obtained by removing one row and one column. Using the expression recursively, we can then write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _1^{(N-k)})-k$$\end{document}N(λ1(N)-λ1(N-k))-k as a sum of k martingale differences. Once this is done, the desired result follows by a martingale CLT. To ensure this recursive argument can continue, we first need to prove that the level repulsion event for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(n)}-\lambda _2^{(n)}$$\end{document}λ1(n)-λ2(n) can be maintained during the iteration process from n=Nn=N to n=N-kn=N-k, as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3. This also requires an iteration to prove, with the aid of the maximal inequality for martingale. The same approach works to show the Gaussianity of other top eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(\lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)})-k$$\end{document}N(λi(N)-λi(N-k))-k, for indices i of order one. Nevertheless, our proof of the level repulsion across the iteration for index i requires the level repulsion for indices smaller than i. This is the technical reason why we stop the extension at a fixed i. We then make one step further by finding an explicit expression for the eigenvectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(n)}$$\end{document}wi(n) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n)}$$\end{document}H(n) and show that in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-n \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}N-n≪N2/3 they are asymptotically aligned, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(n)}, {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(n-1)}\rangle =1+O(N^{-2/3})$$\end{document}⟨wi(n),wi(n-1)⟩=1+O(N-2/3). Iterating this k times and using that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, we conclude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)}, {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N-k)}\rangle \approx 1$$\end{document}⟨wi(N),wi(N-k)⟩≈1.

In the critical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3 we show that the joint distribution of the top eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) is universal (in both real symmetric and complex Hermitian symmetry class), and it is given by the Forrester–Nagao distribution [41] in the complex Hermitian case (see Theorem 2.3 for the explicit distribution). To do this we use a fairly standard long time Green’s function comparison theorem (GFT), i.e. we embed our Wigner matrix H into an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck flow, and show that along this flow the joint statistics of the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) are (asymptotically) unchanged.

Lastly, we discuss the more substantial supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3. In this regime the main ingredient is the almost orthogonality of the eigenvectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)}$$\end{document}wi(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}wi(N-k) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3. More precisely, we first show that1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)}, {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N-k)}\rangle \big |\ll 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|⟨wi(N),wi(N-k)⟩|≪1,by relying on (an extension of) the recent two-resolvents local laws [25, Theorem 3.2], and then we use the Dyson Brownian Motion (DBM) to show that this implies the independence of the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its minor. We now explain in more detail both parts.

We notice that to give an upper bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big |\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)}, {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N-k)}\rangle \big |$$\end{document}|⟨wi(N),wi(N-k)⟩| it is in fact enough to bound21.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \Im (H^{(N)}-z_1)^{-1}\Im (H^{(N-k)}-z_2)^{-1}\rangle \ll \frac{1}{|\Im z_1|}\wedge \frac{1}{|\Im z_2|}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ℑ(H(N)-z1)-1ℑ(H(N-k)-z2)-1⟩≪1|ℑz1|∧1|ℑz2|,for appropriately chosen spectral parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1,z_2\in \textbf{C}\setminus \textbf{R}$$\end{document}z1,z2∈C\R with imaginary part proportional to the local spacing of the eigenvalues. Objects as in (1.2) have been largely studied in random matrix theory in the last few years under the name of multi–resolvent local laws; see for example [24, 27, 28, 31, 33, 58]. To prove (1.2) we rely on the Zigzag strategy (see [24, 31]) which consists of first proving (1.2) for matrices with a fairly large Gaussian component using the method of characteristics (i.e. by studying the evolution of the resolvent along a carefully chosen flow), and then remove this Gaussian component by an additional GFT argument. We point out that the method of characteristics in the context of studying local properties of resolvents of random matrices was first used in [56] to prove edge universality of deformed Wigner matrices. Later, this idea was used to prove eigenvalue rigidity and universality in [15, 49]. Lastly, this approach was extended to study products of resolvents only more recently in [18, 31] and in subsequent papers. Once the bound (1.1) is proven, we can readily conclude the independence of the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its minor by analyzing weakly correlated DBMs following the approach used in [28].

Organization of the paper

We now briefly discuss how the rest of the paper is organized. In Section 2 we state our main results. Then, in Section 3 we present the main steps for the proof of the asymptotic independence in the supercritical regime. Several technical results used in this section are proven in Sections 4–6. In particular, in Section 6 we present a Green’s function comparison (GFT) argument to show the density of matrices with a fairly large Gaussian component in the class of Wigner matrices. This GFT argument is then also used to show universality in the critical regime. Finally, in Section 7 we prove the strong dependence of the edge eigenvalues in the subcritical regime, using a martingale Central Limit Theorem. Several auxiliary results and proofs are deferred to two appendices.

Notations

For positive quantities f, g we write f≲ gf≲g (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=\mathcal {O}(g)$$\end{document}f=O(g)) and f∼ gf∼g if f ≤ C gf≤Cg or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c g\le f\le Cg$$\end{document}cg≤f≤Cg, respectively, for some constants c,C>0c,C>0 which only depend on the constants appearing in the moment condition (see Assumption 2.1). We also frequently use the notation f ≪ gf≪g, which means that f/g → 0f/g→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞.

For any natural number n we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[n]: =\{ 1, 2,\ldots ,n\}$$\end{document}[n]:={1,2,…,n}. Matrix entries are indexed by lowercase Roman letters a, b, c, ... from the beginning of the alphabet. We denote vectors by bold-faced lowercase Roman letters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{x}}, {\boldsymbol{y}}\in \textbf{C}^N$$\end{document}x,y∈CN, or lower case Greek letters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi , \phi \in \textbf{C}^N$$\end{document}ψ,ϕ∈CN, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}N∈N. Vector and matrix norms, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {\boldsymbol{x}}\Vert $$\end{document}‖x‖ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert A\Vert $$\end{document}‖A‖, indicate the usual Euclidean norm and the corresponding induced matrix norm. For any N× NN×N matrix A we use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle A\rangle := N^{-1}\textrm{Tr} A$$\end{document}⟨A⟩:=N-1TrA for its normalized trace. Moreover, for vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{x}}, {\boldsymbol{y}}\in \textbf{C}^N$$\end{document}x,y∈CN we denote their scalar product by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\rangle := \sum _{i} \overline{x}_i y_i$$\end{document}⟨x,y⟩:=∑ix¯iyi.

Finally, we use the concept of “with very high probability” (w.v.h.p.) meaning that for any fixed C>0C>0, the probability of an N-dependent event is bigger than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-N^{-C}$$\end{document}1-N-C for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\ge N_0(C)$$\end{document}N≥N0(C). Moreover, even if not explicitly stated, every estimate involving N is understood to hold for N being sufficiently large. We also introduce the notion of stochastic domination (see e.g. [37]): given two families of non-negative random variables\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X=\left( X^{(N)}(u) : N\in \textbf{N}, u\in U^{(N)} \right) \quad \textrm{and}\quad Y=\left( Y^{(N)}(u) : N\in \textbf{N}, u\in U^{(N)} \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X=X(N)(u):N∈N,u∈U(N)andY=Y(N)(u):N∈N,u∈U(N)indexed by N (and possibly some parameter u in some parameter space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U^{(N)}$$\end{document}U(N)), we say that X is stochastically dominated by Y, if for all ξ , C>0ξ,C>0 we have1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{u\in U^{(N)}} \textbf{P}\left[ X^{(N)}(u)>N^\xi Y^{(N)}(u)\right] \le N^{-C} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supu∈U(N)PX(N)(u)>NξY(N)(u)≤N-Cfor large enough \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N{\ge } N_0(\xi ,C)$$\end{document}N≥N0(ξ,C). In this case we use the notation X≺ YX≺Y or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X{=} \mathcal {O}_\prec (Y)$$\end{document}X=O≺(Y).

Main results

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=(h_{ij})_{i,j=1}^N$$\end{document}H=(hij)i,j=1N be either a real symmetric or a complex Hermitian Wigner matrix, i.e. the entries of H are independent (up to the symmetry constraint) having distribution\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h_{ii}{\mathop {=}\limits ^{d}} \frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\chi _\textrm{d}, \qquad h_{ij}{\mathop {=}\limits ^{d}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\chi _{\textrm{od}},\quad i>j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hii=d1Nχd,hij=d1Nχod,i>j.On the (N-independent) random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _\textrm{d}\in \textbf{R}$$\end{document}χd∈R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{\textrm{od}}\in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}χod∈C we formulate the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.1

Both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _\textrm{d}$$\end{document}χd, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _{\textrm{od}}$$\end{document}χod are centered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{E}\chi _\textrm{d}=\textbf{E}\chi _{\textrm{od}}=0$$\end{document}Eχd=Eχod=0 and have unit variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{E}\chi _\textrm{d}^2= \textbf{E}|\chi _{\textrm{od}}|^2=1$$\end{document}Eχd2=E|χod|2=1. In the complex case we also assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{E}\chi _{\textrm{od}}^2=0$$\end{document}Eχod2=0. Furthermore, we assume the existence of high moments, i.e. for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}p∈N there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_p>0$$\end{document}Cp>0 such that2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}\big [|\chi _\textrm{d}|^p+|\chi _{\textrm{od}}|^p\big ]\le C_p. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[|χd|p+|χod|p]≤Cp.

For k∈ [N]k∈[N], let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{[k]}$$\end{document}H[k] be the minor of H formed by first k rows and first k columns. Similarly, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) denote the minor formed by the last N-kN-k rows and the last N-kN-k columns, i.e2.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H = \begin{pmatrix} H^{[k]} & X_k^*\\ X_k& H^{(N-k)} \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H=H[k]Xk∗XkH(N-k).Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_k$$\end{document}Xk is a block of size (N-k)× k(N-k)×k. Furthermore, we denote the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace \lambda _j^{(N-k)}\rbrace _{j=1}^{N-k}$$\end{document}{λj(N-k)}j=1N-k in decreasing order and the corresponding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-normalized eigenvectors are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)}\in \textbf{C}^{N-k}$$\end{document}wj(N-k)∈CN-k. With a slight abuse of notation, we will identify2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{C}^{N-k}\ni \boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)} \quad \leftrightarrow \quad (\boldsymbol{0}, \boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)}) \in \textbf{C}^N \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CN-k∋wj(N-k)↔(0,wj(N-k))∈CN,where we augmented the original vector with k zeros from the top to form a vector in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}^N$$\end{document}CN, and denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\boldsymbol{0}, \boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}(0,wj(N-k)) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}wj(N-k) as well. For k=0k=0 we sometimes denote H by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N).

Returning to the eigenvalues, note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) is close to 2, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) is close to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2(N-k)^{1/2}N^{-1/2}$$\end{document}2(N-k)1/2N-1/2, both with a fluctuation given by the celebrated Tracy-Widom law [70, 71], on the scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}N-2/3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N-k)^{-2/3}$$\end{document}(N-k)-2/3 respectively. We will consider the regime where k≪ Nk≪N, and thus the fluctuation scale is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}N-2/3 to leading order in both cases. While the distributions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) separately are well known, we now study their correlations, i.e. joint distribution. Moreover, we consider the overlap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)} \big \rangle $$\end{document}〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉 of the top eigenvectors of the original matrix and its minor.

Main results: Decorrelation transition

We study three different regimes: (i)the supercritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.2 in Section 2.1.1;(ii)the critical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.3 in Section 2.1.2;(iii)the subcritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.4 in Section 2.1.2.

(i)the supercritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.2 in Section 2.1.1;

the supercritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.2 in Section 2.1.1;

(ii)the critical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.3 in Section 2.1.2;

the critical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.3 in Section 2.1.2;

(iii)the subcritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.4 in Section 2.1.2.

the subcritical regime with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3, cf. Theorem 2.4 in Section 2.1.2.

Supercritical regime

We begin with the supercritical regime, where we prove that (a) the top eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k), appropriately rescaled, become asymptotically independent in the large N limit, and (b) the top eigenvectors become asymptotically orthogonal in the large N limit. This is the statement of the following theorem.

Theorem 2.2

(Supercritical regime: Asymptotic independence) Fix a (small) constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0>0$$\end{document}ε0>0. Let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}k∈N be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{2/3+\varepsilon _0}\le k\le N^{1-\varepsilon _0}$$\end{document}N2/3+ε0≤k≤N1-ε0. Then we have the following: [Eigenvalues] For any smooth compactly supported test-functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} holds that 2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\textbf{E}\left[ F\big ((\lambda _1^{(N)}-2)N^{2/3}\big ) G\big ((\lambda _1^{(N-k)}-2(1-k/N)^{1/2})(N-k)^{2/3}\big )\right] \\&\quad = \textbf{E}\left[ F\big ((\lambda _1^{(N)}-2)N^{2/3}\big )\right] \textbf{E}\left[ G\big ((\lambda _1^{(N-k)}-2(1-k/N)^{1/2})(N-k)^{2/3}\big )\right] \\ &\qquad + \mathcal {O}\left( N^{-c}\right) \end{aligned}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EF((λ1(N)-2)N2/3)G((λ1(N-k)-2(1-k/N)1/2)(N-k)2/3)=EF((λ1(N)-2)N2/3)EG((λ1(N-k)-2(1-k/N)1/2)(N-k)2/3)+ON-c for some constant c>0c>0 [Eigenvalues] For any smooth compactly supported test-functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,G:\textbf{R}\rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}F,G:R→R it holds that 2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\textbf{E}\left[ F\big ((\lambda _1^{(N)}-2)N^{2/3}\big ) G\big ((\lambda _1^{(N-k)}-2(1-k/N)^{1/2})(N-k)^{2/3}\big )\right] \\&\quad = \textbf{E}\left[ F\big ((\lambda _1^{(N)}-2)N^{2/3}\big )\right] \textbf{E}\left[ G\big ((\lambda _1^{(N-k)}-2(1-k/N)^{1/2})(N-k)^{2/3}\big )\right] \\ &\qquad + \mathcal {O}\left( N^{-c}\right) \end{aligned}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EF((λ1(N)-2)N2/3)G((λ1(N-k)-2(1-k/N)1/2)(N-k)2/3)=EF((λ1(N)-2)N2/3)EG((λ1(N-k)-2(1-k/N)1/2)(N-k)2/3)+ON-c for some constant c>0c>0 which does not depend on N.
