[Eigenvectors] Using the identification (2.3), it holds that 2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}\big \rangle \big |^2 \prec \frac{N^{2/3}}{k} \le N^{-\varepsilon _0} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|2≺N2/3k≤N-ε0, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}$$\end{document}w1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}w1(N-k) are almost orthogonal.

[Eigenvectors] Using the identification (2.3), it holds that 2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}\big \rangle \big |^2 \prec \frac{N^{2/3}}{k} \le N^{-\varepsilon _0} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|2≺N2/3k≤N-ε0, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}$$\end{document}w1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}w1(N-k) are almost orthogonal.

We formulated Theorem 2.2 (a) only for the top eigenvalue, but our proof also gives a similar result for the joint distribution of any p-tuple of top eigenvalues, i.e. the collection of appropriately rescaled p-tuples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{i_1}^{(N)}, \ldots \lambda _{i_p}^{(N)})$$\end{document}(λi1(N),…λip(N)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{j_1}^{(N-k)}, \ldots \lambda _{j_p}^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}(λj1(N-k),…λjp(N-k)) are asymptotically independent if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_1, \ldots i_p, j_1, \ldots , j_p\le N^\varepsilon $$\end{document}i1,…ip,j1,…,jp≤Nε. Similarly, also the top eigenvectors considered in Theorem 2.2 (b) are asymptotically orthogonal, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_j^{(N-k)}, \boldsymbol{w}_i^{(N)}\big \rangle \big | \ll 1$$\end{document}|〈wj(N-k),wi(N)〉|≪1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \le N^\varepsilon $$\end{document}i,j≤Nε.

Critical regime

Next, we turn to the critical regime, where we prove that the joint one-point correlation function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) near the edge is universal. We expect that the corresponding overlap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big | \big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}\big \rangle \big |^2$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|2 also has a nontrivial and universal distribution. However, since the limiting distribution is not known, we do not pursue this question further in this paper.

Theorem 2.3

(Critical regime: Nontrivial joint distribution.) Let H be a complex Hermitian Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1. Fix a constant α >0α>0 and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k:=\lfloor \alpha N^{2/3}\rfloor $$\end{document}k:=⌊αN2/3⌋. Denote the joint one-point correlation function of the eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \lambda _j^{(N)}\}_{j=1}^N$$\end{document}{λj(N)}j=1N of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H=H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \lambda _j^{(N-k)}\}_{j=1}^{N-k}$$\end{document}{λj(N-k)}j=1N-k of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_\alpha ^{(N)}:\textbf{R}^2\rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}pα(N):R2→R, i.e. it is defined by the identity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}\frac{1}{N(N-k)} \sum _{i,j} f(\lambda _i^{(N)}, \lambda _j^{(N-k)}) = \int _{\textbf{R}}\int _\textbf{R}p_\alpha ^{(N)}\left( x,y\right) f(x,y) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E1N(N-k)∑i,jf(λi(N),λj(N-k))=∫R∫Rpα(N)x,yf(x,y)dxdyfor any smooth compactly supported function f. For any parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X, Y\in \textbf{R}$$\end{document}X,Y∈R introduce the edge-scaling\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x:= 2+N^{-2/3}X,\quad y:= 2(1-k/N)^{1/2} + N^{-1/2} (N-k)^{-1/6} Y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x:=2+N-2/3X,y:=2(1-k/N)1/2+N-1/2(N-k)-1/6Y.Then, in the sense of weak convergence, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty } N^{2/3} p_\alpha ^{(N)}\left( x,y\right) \\ & \quad =4\det \begin{pmatrix} \int _0^{+\infty } \textrm{Ai}(X+u)\textrm{Ai}(X+u)\textrm{d}u& \!\!\!\!\!\!\int _0^{+\infty } \textrm{e}^{-\alpha u} \textrm{Ai}(X+u)\textrm{Ai}(Y+u)\textrm{d}u\\ & \\ -\int _{-\infty }^{0} \textrm{e}^{\alpha u} \textrm{Ai}(X+u)\textrm{Ai}(Y+u)\textrm{d}u& \!\!\!\!\!\!\int _0^{+\infty } \textrm{Ai}(Y+u)\textrm{Ai}(Y+u)\textrm{d}u \end{pmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞N2/3pα(N)x,y=4det∫0+∞Ai(X+u)Ai(X+u)du∫0+∞e-αuAi(X+u)Ai(Y+u)du-∫-∞0eαuAi(X+u)Ai(Y+u)du∫0+∞Ai(Y+u)Ai(Y+u)du,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Ai}$$\end{document}Ai is the Airy function.

The limiting distribution in Theorem 2.3 was explicitly computed by Forrester and Nagao for GUE matrices [41, Eq. (5.35)]. We also point out that Theorem 2.3 is stated specifically for the complex Hermitian case. This choice stems from our proof strategy which relies on comparing H with a GUE matrix and applying the version of Theorem 2.3 established for GUE in [41, Proposition 5]. However, our method extends to the real symmetric case. In fact, we prove that the joint distribution is universal in both symmetry classes, just the explicit formula analogous to [41, Eq. (5.35)] is not (yet) available for GOE. Moreover, our proof shows that universality also holds for the r-point correlation functions at the edge regime (for any finite r) even though we wrote up only the r=1r=1 case. In the GUE case the process is determinantal, so an explicit formula for the r-point function is available, see [41, Eq. (5.35)].

Subcritical regime

Finally, we turn to the subcritical regime, where we prove that the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)} - \lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ1(N-k) is approximately Gaussian with variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}/N \ll N^{-2/3}$$\end{document}k/N≪N-2/3, i.e. much smaller than their individual fluctuation scale. This indicates the strong correlation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k). Moreover, we show that the top eigenvectors are almost aligned, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big |\big \langle \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N)}, \boldsymbol{w}_1^{(N-k)}\big \rangle \big | \approx 1$$\end{document}|〈w1(N),w1(N-k)〉|≈1.

We formulated Theorem 2.4 only for the top eigenvalue and top eigenvector, but our proof in Section 7 (see Propositions 7.1–7.2) gives analogous results when replacing the index i=1i=1 of the λ λ’s and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}$$\end{document}w’s by some i ≤ Ci≤C for an N-independent constant C > 0C>0. We can even show that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda _i^{(N)} - \lambda _i^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}(λi(N)-λi(N-k))’s are asymptotically independent.

Remark 2.5

In our statement of the normal fluctuation (2.6) we subtracted k from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(\lambda _1^{(N)} - \lambda _1^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}N(λ1(N)-λ1(N-k)) and not exactly its expectation. The discrepancy is minor, in fact we expect that2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N\textbf{E}(\lambda _1^{(N)} - \lambda _1^{(N-k)})=k+o(\sqrt{k}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}NE(λ1(N)-λ1(N-k))=k+o(k)holds for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ll k \le N^{2/3-\varepsilon _0}$$\end{document}1≪k≤N2/3-ε0, but strictly speaking this does not follow from existing results in the literature.

In fact, in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge N^{\epsilon _0}$$\end{document}k≥Nϵ0, [66, Theorem 1.3] proves, for any ω >0ω>0, an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1/3+\omega }$$\end{document}N-1/3+ω speed of convergence of the distribution function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{P}( N^{2/3}(\lambda ^{(N)}_1-2)\ge r)$$\end{document}P(N2/3(λ1(N)-2)≥r) to the Tracy-Widom distribution. This result is formulated for any fixed r, which does not directly imply the same speed of convergence for the expectation. However, we believe that this theorem can easily be extended to any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\ge -C(\log N)^{1/3}$$\end{document}r≥-C(logN)1/3, for some large C>0C>0. This extension, together with standard moderate deviation bounds on the tail would imply that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}N^{2/3}(\lambda _1^{(N)} - 2 ) = \kappa + O(N^{-1/3 + \omega }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EN2/3(λ1(N)-2)=κ+O(N-1/3+ω),for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in \textbf{R}$$\end{document}κ∈R (in fact κ κ is the first moment of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TW_\beta $$\end{document}TWβ distribution). By simple scaling, a similar asymptotic holds for the largest eigenvalue of the minor\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}(N-k)^{2/3}(\lambda _1^{(N-k)} - 2 \sqrt{1-k/N} ) = \kappa + O(N^{-1/3 + \omega }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(N-k)2/3(λ1(N-k)-21-k/N)=κ+O(N-1/3+ω).From these relations, simple algebra shows (2.7).

Extension towards the bulk

In Section 2.1 we considered the decorrelation transition strictly in the edge regime. More precisely, we studied the few top eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i$$\end{document}λi, with i∼ 1i∼1, of a Wigner matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its minor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k), obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) by removing the first k rows and columns. We proved that their correlation exhibits a phase transition when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2/3; for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ll N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2/3 the largest eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) are strongly correlated, while in the complementary regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3 they are asymptotically independent.

While in Section 2.1 we studied only the regime i∼ 1i∼1, we expect that a similar phase transition occurs in the entire complement of the bulk of the spectrum. We claim that for any exponent a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1) and eigenvalue index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na we have a transition (from strong dependence to independence) on a scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2(1-a)/3. The situation in the bulk, a=1a=1, is very different and will be explained separately at the end (see also Figure 2).Fig. 2(A): Decorrelation transition in the intermediate regime a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1). The figure depicts the shrinking spectrum of the minors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k); the dashed line their largest eigenvalue. We consider a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1), fix an index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na and track the trajectory of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) as k decreases. This trajectory crosses the solid red line at the level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k(a)\sim N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k(a)∼N2(1-a)/3, marking the phase transition: for k≪ k(a)k≪k(a), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N) remain highly correlated, while for k≫ k(a)k≫k(a), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) becomes essentially independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N). For a=1a=1, i.e. to the left of the dotted line (bulk regime), the eigenvalues remain correlated for any k. (B): Individual eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its first two minors in the extreme edge regime and in the bulk regime. In the edge regime, the i-th eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)},\lambda _i^{(N-1)}, \lambda _i^{(N-2)}\ldots $$\end{document}λi(N),λi(N-1),λi(N-2)… stick to each other indicating very strong correlation. In the bulk regime the only correlation present is the one enforced by interlacing.

(A): Decorrelation transition in the intermediate regime a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1). The figure depicts the shrinking spectrum of the minors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k); the dashed line their largest eigenvalue. We consider a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1), fix an index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na and track the trajectory of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) as k decreases. This trajectory crosses the solid red line at the level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k(a)\sim N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k(a)∼N2(1-a)/3, marking the phase transition: for k≪ k(a)k≪k(a), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N) remain highly correlated, while for k≫ k(a)k≫k(a), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N-k)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N-k) becomes essentially independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(N)}_i$$\end{document}λi(N). For a=1a=1, i.e. to the left of the dotted line (bulk regime), the eigenvalues remain correlated for any k. (B): Individual eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and its first two minors in the extreme edge regime and in the bulk regime. In the edge regime, the i-th eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)},\lambda _i^{(N-1)}, \lambda _i^{(N-2)}\ldots $$\end{document}λi(N),λi(N-1),λi(N-2)… stick to each other indicating very strong correlation. In the bulk regime the only correlation present is the one enforced by interlacing.

We now explain how to obtain the threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2(1-a)/3 for a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1). We do not present the mathematical details for brevity, but just explain the general strategy. We separate the argument into two parts: we first show that the eigenvalues corresponding to indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na are (asymptotically) independent for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2(1-a)/3 and then that they are strongly correlated for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ll N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2(1-a)/3.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2(1-a)/3, we follow a strategy similar to Section 3, i.e. we show the independence of the eigenvalues using a Dyson Brownian motion (DBM) argument. More precisely, we couple the time evolution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(N)(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N-k)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(N-k)(t) under a Brownian matrix flow with two fully independent DBM flows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i^{(1)}(t), \mu _i^{(2)}(t)$$\end{document}μi(1)(t),μi(2)(t) showing that for larger times t they are close to each other. To control the coupling, technically this requires following analogue of the eigenvector overlap bound (cf. (3.1)):2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)},{\boldsymbol{w}}_j^{(N-k)}\rangle \big |^2\prec \frac{N^{2(1-a)/3}}{k} \wedge 1, \qquad \quad i,j\sim N^a. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|⟨wi(N),wj(N-k)⟩|2≺N2(1-a)/3k∧1,i,j∼Na.The proof of (2.8) is very similar to the one in Section 4.2 below, once it is taken into consideration that the local eigenvalue density is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim N^{(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}∼N(1-a)/3, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na. The bound (2.8) shows that the eigenvectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) are asymptotically orthogonal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\langle {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N)},{\boldsymbol{w}}_j^{(N-k)}\rangle |\ll 1$$\end{document}|⟨wi(N),wj(N-k)⟩|≪1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2(1-a)/3. Armed with this bound as an input, we can then proceed with a DBM argument similar to the one in Section 5. The only difference is that in the current case we cannot directly rely on the results from [15, 55], which are formulated only in the regime i∼ 1i∼1. However, inspecting their proof, it is clear that similar arguments can be extended to the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na, for a∈ (0,1)a∈(0,1). This gives the desired independence when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2(1-a)/3.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ll N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k≪N2(1-a)/3, we can follow a strategy similar to Section 7. As the level repulsion scale for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(n)}-\lambda _{i+1}^{(n)}$$\end{document}λi(n)-λi+1(n), for n∈ [N-k, N]n∈[N-k,N], should now be changed to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{-(2+a)/3-\delta }$$\end{document}n-(2+a)/3-δ, the error term in each step of the recursion analogous to (7.11) will be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{(a-1)/3+C\delta }$$\end{document}N(a-1)/3+Cδ instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1/3+C\delta }$$\end{document}N-1/3+Cδ. Hence, after k steps of the iteration, the error term will be of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k N^{(a-1)/3+C\delta }$$\end{document}kN(a-1)/3+Cδ, as an extension of the error in (7.24). Since we will further renormalize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N(\lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}N(λi(N)-λi(N-k)) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}$$\end{document}k in order to get the CLT, we require \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}N^{(a-1)/3+C\delta }$$\end{document}kN(a-1)/3+Cδ to be negligible. This leads to the threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}N2(1-a)/3 from the subcritical side. It is nevertheless unclear at this moment how to extend our argument on level repulsion across the iteration from a fixed N-independent i to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^{a}$$\end{document}i∼Na. Together with the explanation in the previous paragraph, this shows a transition at a scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^{2(1-a)/3}$$\end{document}k∼N2(1-a)/3, with a∈ [0,1)a∈[0,1).

One might naively think that this phase transition behavior extends also to the purely bulk regime. However, the situation in the regime a=1a=1 is very different. In fact, one can see that when i∼ Ni∼N single eigenvalues are non-trivially correlated for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^b$$\end{document}k∼Nb, with b∈ [0,1)b∈[0,1), while eigenvalue gaps are asymptotically independent for any k≫ 1k≫1. The independence of the gaps follows analogously to the DBM–argument above (see also [30, Section 4] and [25, Remark 2.8]). Single eigenvalues are subject to interlacing which, for example, restricts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N-1)}$$\end{document}λi(N-1) to be in the 1/N-vicinity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}$$\end{document}λi(N) for i∼ Ni∼N. Since the individual eigenvalues fluctuate on the bigger \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{\log N}/N$$\end{document}logN/N scale (see [19, Theorem 1.6], [20, Corollary 1.9], [60, Corollary 1.3], and [53, Theorem 1.3]), this restriction is severe, leading to strong correlation. Surprisingly, this correlation does not disappear when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λi(N-k) is compared with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}$$\end{document}λi(N) for large k; interlacing alone cannot explain this.

To see that single eigenvalues are correlated up to k≪ Nk≪N one can use that by [15, Theorem 3.1] we have2.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _i^{(N)}(t)=\mu _i^{(1)}(t)+F_1(t,N), \quad \lambda _i^{(N-k)}(t)=\mu _i^{(2)}(t)+F_2(t,N-k), \quad i\sim N,\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λi(N)(t)=μi(1)(t)+F1(t,N),λi(N-k)(t)=μi(2)(t)+F2(t,N-k),i∼N,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/N\ll t \ll 1$$\end{document}1/N≪t≪1. Here the random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1$$\end{document}F1 depends only on the initial conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}(0), \mu _i^{(1)}(0)$$\end{document}λi(N)(0),μi(1)(0) and it is such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|F_1|\prec 1/N$$\end{document}|F1|≺1/N, and similarly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_2$$\end{document}F2. In fact, these quantities are mesoscopic linear statistics of eigenvalues and they represent collective fluctuations of many eigenvalues effectively on an energy scale of order t. Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i^{(1)}(t),\mu _i^{(2)}(t)$$\end{document}μi(1)(t),μi(2)(t) are independent of each other for any t≥ 0t≥0. From (2.9) we thus show that the dependence between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}(t), \lambda _i^{(N-k)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(N)(t),λi(N-k)(t) lies purely in the dependence of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_i$$\end{document}Fi (we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\sim N^{-1+\omega }$$\end{document}t∼N-1+ω, for a small ω >0ω>0). However, using resolvent methods, one can prove that2.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Cov}\big [F_1,F_2]\sim \frac{\log (t+k/N)}{N^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cov[F1,F2]∼log(t+k/N)N2.Recall that the natural fluctuation scale of the bulk eigenvalues is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{\log N}/N$$\end{document}logN/N. Therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(N)(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N-k)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(N-k)(t) would become independent only when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Cov}\big [F_1,F_2] \ll (\log N)/N^2$$\end{document}Cov[F1,F2]≪(logN)/N2, i.e. when b=1b=1 (in the scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\sim N^b$$\end{document}k∼Nb) and they are genuinely correlated for any b∈ [0,1)b∈[0,1). Strictly speaking (2.10) is proven only for order one test function (i.e. when t∼ 1t∼1) in [13, Proposition 1] (see also [14, Proposition 1]); however, we believe that using the method of characteristics for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_i$$\end{document}Fi one can obtain (2.10) (see e.g. [16, Section 5], [32, Sections 5-6], and [54, Section 7]).

We point out that the mesoscopic terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1, F_2$$\end{document}F1,F2 in (2.9) are present in every regime not only in the bulk and their size is essentially of order 1/N everywhere (see, e.g., [47, Theorem 2.3] restricted to the bulk, as well as [29, Theorem 2.4] and [57, Theorems 2.10-2.11] valid for the entire spectrum including the relevant edge regimes but only for compactly supported mesoscopic functions). The main difference between bulk indices i∼ Ni∼N and indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\sim N^a$$\end{document}i∼Na, a<1a<1, closer to the edge is that in the latter case the natural fluctuation scale of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i$$\end{document}λi is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-(2+a)/3}$$\end{document}N-(2+a)/3, i.e. away from the bulk it is much bigger than the natural size of the order 1/N of the mesoscopic terms. Therefore the nontrivial correlation between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1, F_2$$\end{document}F1,F2 (still present away from the bulk) becomes negligible on the relevant fluctuation scale.

Supercritical and critical regime: Proofs of Theorems 2.2–2.3

In this section, we provide the proofs of Theorems 2.2–2.3.

Proof of Theorem 2.2

In this section we focus on the supercritical regime, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\gg N^{2/3}$$\end{document}k≫N2/3. As the first step, we show that in this regime the eigenvectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) become nearly orthogonal to each other, as stated in Proposition 3.1 below. The proof is given in Section 4.

Proposition 3.1

(Step 1: Bound on the eigenvector overlaps) Fix (small) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0>0$$\end{document}ε0>0 and let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1. This immediately proves Theorem 2.2 (b) and, at the same time, serves as the basis for the following Dyson Brownian Motion (DBM) analysis for the eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ1(N-k) in the second step.

Indeed, the overlap bound from Proposition 3.1 shows that the driving Brownian motions in (3.4) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \le N^{\varepsilon _1}$$\end{document}i≤Nε1 are almost independent. This allows to infer that after sufficiently long time t, the top eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_t^{(N_1)}$$\end{document}Ht(N1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_t^{(N_2)}$$\end{document}Ht(N2) are essentially independent.

Proposition 3.2

(Step 2: Approximate independence for Gaussian divisible ensemble) Let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1. 100c}$$\end{document}t≥N-1/3+100c and any smooth compactly supported test-functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} The same approximate factorization property as in (3.6) holds also for the joint distribution of any p-tuple of top-eigenvalues i.e. the collection of appropriately rescaled p-tuples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{i_1}^{(1)}(t), \ldots \lambda _{i_p}^{(1)}(t))$$\end{document}(λi1(1)(t),…λip(1)(t)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{j_1}^{(2)}(t), \ldots \lambda _{j_p}^{(2)}(t))$$\end{document}(λj1(2)(t),…λjp(2)(t)) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_1, \ldots i_p, j_1, \ldots , j_p\le N^\varepsilon $$\end{document}i1,…ip,j1,…,jp≤Nε, and test functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F, G : \textbf{R}^p \rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}F,G:Rp→R.

The proof of Proposition 3.2 is given in Section 5 and it gives Theorem 2.2 (a) for ensembles with a Gaussian component of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1/3+\varepsilon }$$\end{document}N-1/3+ε. As the final step for proving Theorem 2.2 (a), we remove the Gaussian component introduced by (3.4) via a Green function comparison (GFT) argument, which is proven in Section 6.

Proposition 3.3

(Step 3: Removing the Gaussian component) Let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1, fix a (small) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0 > 0$$\end{document}ε0>0 and take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge N^{2/3 + \varepsilon _0}$$\end{document}k≥N2/3+ε0. Assume that for some small constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1, c > 0$$\end{document}ε1,c>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \le N^{\varepsilon _1}$$\end{document}i,j≤Nε1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \ge N^{-1/3 + 100c}$$\end{document}t≥N-1/3+100c, and any smooth compactly supported test-functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,G:\textbf{R}\rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}F,G:R→R, we have that (3.6) holds. Then (3.6) holds for time t=0t=0 as well.

Moreover, the same conclusion holds also for the joint distribution of any p-tuple of top-eigenvalues i.e. the collection of appropriately rescaled p-tuples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{i_1}^{(1)}(t), \ldots \lambda _{i_p}^{(1)}(t))$$\end{document}(λi1(1)(t),…λip(1)(t)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \lambda _{j_1}^{(2)}(t), \ldots \lambda _{j_p}^{(2)}(t))$$\end{document}(λj1(2)(t),…λjp(2)(t)) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_1, \ldots i_p, j_1, \ldots , j_p\le N^\varepsilon $$\end{document}i1,…ip,j1,…,jp≤Nε, and test functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F, G : \textbf{R}^p \rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}F,G:Rp→R.

Combining Proposition 3.2 with Proposition 3.3, we immediately conclude the proof of Theorem 2.2 (a) and thus the whole proof of Theorem 2.2. □ □

Proof of Theorem 2.3

The proof of Theorem 2.3 entirely relies on the following comparison argument, proven in Section 6.

Proposition 3.4

(Comparison with the Gaussian ensemble) Let H be a real symmetric or complex Hermitian Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1. Fix a constant α > 0α>0 and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k := \lfloor \alpha N^{2/3} \rfloor $$\end{document}k:=⌊αN2/3⌋. Moreover, fix a small ɛ > 0ε>0 and consider the joint r-point correlation functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) in the edge regime and denote it by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{r, \alpha }^{(N)} : \textbf{R}^{2r} \rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}pr,α(N):R2r→R. Denote the analog for the GOE/GUE (corresponding to the symmetry class of H) ensemble by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{p}_{r, \alpha }^{(N)} : \textbf{R}^{2r} \rightarrow \textbf{R}$$\end{document}p~r,α(N):R2r→R.

Then, in the sense of weak convergence (i.e. tested against smooth compactly supported test functions) , it holds that3.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{N \rightarrow \infty } N^{2r/3} \left( p_{r, \alpha }^{(N)}(x_1, ... , x_r, y_1, ... , y_r) - \widetilde{p}_{r, \alpha }^{(N)}(x_1, ... , x_r, y_1, ... , y_r)\right) = 0 \,.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞N2r/3pr,α(N)(x1,...,xr,y1,...,yr)-p~r,α(N)(x1,...,xr,y1,...,yr)=0.

Given Proposition 3.4, Theorem 2.3 readily follows by invoking the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{2/3}\widetilde{p}_{1, \alpha }^{(N)}(x, y)$$\end{document}N2/3p~1,α(N)(x,y) in the complex Hermitian case. □ □

Eigenvector overlaps: Proof of Proposition 3.1

In this section, we prove the overlap bound in Proposition 3.1 via a suitable two-resolvent local for the resolvents of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k). The key idea to relate the two resolvents with each other is to view the resolvent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) as the resolvent of a deformation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H(N-k) via Schur complement formula.

More precisely, taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_1, z_2\in \textbf{H}$$\end{document}z1,z2∈H, we will extract the overlap bound (3.1) from an upper bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \langle \Im (H^{(N)}-z_1)^{-1}\Im F(z_2)\rangle \right| $$\end{document}⟨ℑ(H(N)-z1)-1ℑF(z2)⟩, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(z_2)$$\end{document}F(z2) is extended from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^{(N-k)}-z_2)^{-1} \in \textbf{C}^{(N-k) \times (N-k)}$$\end{document}(H(N-k)-z2)-1∈C(N-k)×(N-k) to an N× NN×N matrix by writing zeros in the first k rows and columns. Then, using Schur complement formula for the minor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H^{(N)}-z_1)^{-1}$$\end{document}(H(N)-z1)-1 formed by the last (N-k)(N-k) rows and columns we find that4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \Im (H-z_1)^{-1}\Im F(z_2)\rangle = \frac{1}{N}\textrm{Tr}\left[ \Im \left( H^{(N-k)}-D_k - z_1\right) ^{-1} \Im (H^{(N-k)}-z_2)^{-1} \right] \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ℑ(H-z1)-1ℑF(z2)⟩=1NTrℑH(N-k)-Dk-z1-1ℑ(H(N-k)-z2)-1where the deformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_k$$\end{document}Dk is given by4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_k:=X_k(H^{[k]}-z_1)^{-1}X_k^*. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dk:=Xk(H[k]-z1)-1Xk∗.In order to estimate (4.1), we will first collect some preliminary information on the deformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_k$$\end{document}Dk in Section 4.1 and afterwards, in Section 4.2, show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|(4.1)| \prec N^{2/3}/k \wedge 1$$\end{document}|(4.1)|≺N2/3/k∧1, yielding the desired bound (3.1).

Preliminaries: Analysis of the deformation

To analyze \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_k$$\end{document}Dk, we first establish the concentration of the sample covariance matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_kX_k^*$$\end{document}XkXk∗ around the identity matrix in Lemma 4.1. We then outline several important properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_k$$\end{document}Dk in Lemma 4.2, which are essential for studying (4.1). The proofs of both lemmas are postponed to Section 4.3.

Lemma 4.1

(Concentration of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_kX_k^*$$\end{document}XkXk∗ around the identity matrix) Fix (small) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0, c>0$$\end{document}ε0,c>0. Lemma 4.3

\usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\langle \Im \left( H^{(N-k)}-\Re D_k - z_1\right) ^{-1} \Im (H^{(N-k)}-z_2)^{-1} \right\rangle \prec \left( \left\langle (\Re D_k - \left\langle \Re D_k\right\rangle )^2\right\rangle \right) ^{-1},\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℑH(N-k)-ℜDk-z1-1ℑ(H(N-k)-z2)-1≺(ℜDk-ℜDk)2-1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot \rangle $$\end{document}⟨·⟩ stands for the normalized trace of an (N-k)× (N-k)(N-k)×(N-k) matrix, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot \rangle =(N-k)^{-1}\textrm{Tr}[\cdot ]$$\end{document}⟨·⟩=(N-k)-1Tr[·]. The proof of (4.12) relies on the two-resolvent local law from Proposition A.1 and is provided in Appendix A.3. Finally, applying (4.5) to bound the right-hand-side of (4.12) from above and combining this with (4.11), we complete the proof of Proposition 3.1. □ □

Proof of the lemmas from section 4.1

Proof of Lemma 4.3

Firstly notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert (H-\textrm{i}\eta (I+S))^{-1}\Vert \le 1/\eta $$\end{document}‖(H-iη(I+S))-1‖≤1/η. Weakly correlated Dyson Brownian motions at the edge: Proof of Proposition 3.2

In order to simplify the notations we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{\omega _A}\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}NωA∈N. However, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{{\boldsymbol{b}}}(t)$$\end{document}b_(t) is still a martingale. Moreover, by Doob’s martingale representation theorem from [51, Theorem 18.12] there exists a standard \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{{\boldsymbol{\beta }}}(t)$$\end{document}β_(t) such that5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Proposition 5.1

(Comparison with independent eigenvalues) Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\omega _A<\omega _0/2<1$$\end{document}0<ωA<ω0/2<1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{\textrm{ov}}>0$$\end{document}ωov>0.
