Proof of Proposition 3.2

The approximate independence in (3.6) immediately follows from (5.5), the independence of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i^{(l)}$$\end{document}μi(l)’s (by construction of the processes in (5.3)), and the smoothness of F, G, which allows us to perturb their arguments by an amount of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-\omega }$$\end{document}N-ω without essentially changing their value. □ □

It now remains to give the proof of Proposition 5.1.

Proof of Proposition 5.1

We independently verify (5.5) for l=1l=1 and l=2l=2 without relying on the connection between (3.5) for these two values of parameter l. Since the arguments for l=1l=1 and l=2l=2 are identical, we further focus on the case l=1l=1 in our analysis of (3.5) and (5.3). Henceforth, we omit the superscript in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{(1)}_j(t)$$\end{document}λj(1)(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(1)}_j(t)$$\end{document}μj(1)(t) for simplicity. Our proof closely follows [15] (see also [55]), so in order to avoid redundancy we will show how the main steps in [15] are modified to suit the current setting.

First of all, we interpolate between the weakly correlated DBM (3.5) and the comparison process (5.3) with independent driving Brownian motions by introducing the following one-parameter family of flows: for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [0,1]$$\end{document}α∈[0,1] we set (recall β =1β=1 for real symmetric case, β =2β=2 for complex Hermitian case)5.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}z_i(t,\alpha )= & \alpha \sqrt{\frac{\beta }{2N}} \textrm{d}b_i(t)\nonumber \\ & + (1-\alpha ) \sqrt{\frac{\beta }{2N}} \textrm{d}\beta _i(t) + \frac{1}{N}\sum _{j\ne i} \frac{1}{z_j(t,\alpha ) - z_i(t,\alpha )}, \quad i\in [N],\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dzi(t,α)=αβ2Ndbi(t)+(1-α)β2Ndβi(t)+1N∑j≠i1zj(t,α)-zi(t,α),i∈[N],with initial conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(0,\alpha ):=\alpha \lambda _i(0) + (1-\alpha )\mu _i(0)$$\end{document}zi(0,α):=αλi(0)+(1-α)μi(0). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i(t,0)=\mu _i(t)$$\end{document}zi(t,0)=μi(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_j(t,1)=\lambda _j(t)$$\end{document}zj(t,1)=λj(t) for all t≥ 0t≥0. The process (5.6) is well-posed in the sense particle collisions almost surely do not occur, i.e.5.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{P}\left[ z_1(t,\alpha )< z_2(t,\alpha )<\cdots < z_N(t,\alpha ), \quad \forall t\in [0,1], \,\forall \alpha \in [0,1]\right] =1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pz1(t,α)<z2(t,α)<⋯<zN(t,α),∀t∈[0,1],∀α∈[0,1]=1.The proof of (5.7) is analogous to the proof of [22, Theorem 5.2] adapted to the current context as outlined in [26, Section 7.6]. We point out that in the real case (β =1β=1) to ensure the well-posedness of the interpolation process we need to slightly change the flow in (5.6) by dividing the stochastic terms by factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1+N^{-\omega }$$\end{document}1+N-ω, for a sufficiently small ω >0ω>0 (see e.g. [22, Eq. (4.13)], [26, Eq. (7.22)]).

Another important property of (5.6) essential for the proof of (5.5) is the rigidity. To explain this phenomenon let us denote the Stieltjes transform of the empirical particle density by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_N(z,t,\alpha ):=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N \frac{1}{z_j(t,\alpha )-z}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}mN(z,t,α):=1N∑j=1N1zj(t,α)-z.Additionally, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{sc}$$\end{document}msc stand for the Stieltjes transform of the semicircular distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{sc}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi }\sqrt{[4-x^2]_+}$$\end{document}ρsc(x)=12π[4-x2]+. Recall the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _j^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}γj(N-k) from (4.7) and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _j:=\gamma _j^{(N)}$$\end{document}γj:=γj(N).

Then for any (large) L>0L>0 and (small) ɛ >0ε>0 we have5.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sup _{\vert \Re z\vert \le L}\sup _{N^{-1+\varepsilon }\le \rho _{sc}(z)\Im z\le L} \sup _{\alpha \in [0,1]}\sup _{t\in [0,t_1]} N\Im z\vert m_N(z,t,\alpha )-m_{sc}(z)\vert \prec 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup|ℜz|≤LsupN-1+ε≤ρsc(z)ℑz≤Lsupα∈[0,1]supt∈[0,t1]Nℑz|mN(z,t,α)-msc(z)|≺1,5.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sup _{j\le N^{\omega _A}}\sup _{\alpha \in [0,1]}\sup _{t\in [0,t_1]} N^{2/3}\vert z_j(t,\alpha )-\gamma _j\vert \prec 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supj≤NωAsupα∈[0,1]supt∈[0,t1]N2/3|zj(t,α)-γj|≺1.The rigidity estimate (5.9) follows as a standard consequence of (5.8) (see e.g. [36, Section 5]). The proof of (5.8) is identical to that of [49, Theorem 3.1]. The only modification required is in the proof of [49, Proposition 3.8], as the driving Brownian motions in (5.6) are weakly correlated. This correlation introduces cross-terms between different particles, contributing to the quadratic variation in [49, Eq. (3.33)] and to the sum in the left-hand-side of [49, Eq. (3.32)]. However, this additional terms are negligible, which is ensured by (5.4) combined with the choice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _A<\omega _{\textrm{ov}}/2$$\end{document}ωA<ωov/2 (see [16, Section 4.4] for a similar argument in the bulk regime).

Having (5.7)–(5.9) in hand, the rest of the proof closely follows7 [15, Sections 2.2–2.3]. The only complication compared to [15] is that an additional term containing the quadratic variation process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}b_i-\textrm{d}\beta _i$$\end{document}dbi-dβi appears in the right-hand-side of [55, Eq. (2.3)] (see also [16, Lemma 4.19] for an analogous term in a slightly different model). However, a similar estimate has been already conducted for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}\xi _{1,i}$$\end{document}dξ1,i in [28, Eqs. (7.92)–(7.95)] and in the proof of [16, Lemma 4.20]. This completes the proof of Proposition 5.1.

□ □

Green function comparison: Proof of Propositions 3.3 and 3.4

In this section, we provide the proofs of Propositions 3.3 and 3.4 based on a Green function comparison (GFT) argument. At the spectral edge, it is a well established approach (see, e.g., [39, Section 17] or [36]) to directly compare the matrix of interest with the corresponding GOE/GUE ensemble (a so called long time GFT). We follow this approach here as well, the only novelty being that now the resolvents of two different Wigner matrices are involved in the GFT (see Proposition 6.1 below).

To prepare the formulation of the GFT, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) be a Wigner matrix and consider the matrix valued Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process6.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}\widehat{H}^{(N)}_t = - \frac{\widehat{H}^{(N)}_t}{2} \textrm{d}t+\frac{\textrm{d}\widehat{B}^{(N)}_t}{\sqrt{N}} \,, \qquad \widehat{H}_0^{(N)} = H^{(N)} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dH^t(N)=-H^t(N)2dt+dB^t(N)N,H^0(N)=H(N),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{B}^{(N)}_t$$\end{document}B^t(N) is a real symmetric or complex Hermitian standard matrix valued Brownian motion of the same symmetry class as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(N)}$$\end{document}H(N) (see, e.g., below (3.2)) and we observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}^{(N)}_\infty = \textrm{G}\beta \textrm{E}$$\end{document}H^∞(N)=GβE. Moreover, similarly to (3.3), we consider the time-dependent analog of the minor process defined in (2.2) and denote the resolvent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}^{(N-k)}_t$$\end{document}H^t(N-k) for k ∈ [N]k∈[N] at a spectral parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \textbf{C}\setminus \textbf{R}$$\end{document}z∈C\R by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_t^{(N-k)}(z) := \big (\widehat{H}_t^{(N-k)} - z\big )^{-1}$$\end{document}Gt(N-k)(z):=(H^t(N-k)-z)-1.

Proposition 6.1

(Green function comparison at the edge) Fix δ > 0δ>0. Take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}N∈N and k ∈ [N]k∈[N] with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \le N^{1 - \delta }$$\end{document}k≤N1-δ. 2| \le C N^{-2/3 + \varepsilon } \quad \text {and} \quad |\widetilde{E}_i - 2\sqrt{1 - k/N}| \le C N^{-2/3 + \varepsilon } \,, \quad i \in [2] \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Ei-2|≤CN-2/3+εand|E~i-21-k/N|≤CN-2/3+ε,i∈[2],and setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta := N^{-2/3 - \varepsilon }$$\end{document}η:=N-2/3-ε, we have6.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg | & \textbf{E}f\bigg ( N \int _{E_1}^{E_2} \textrm{d}x \, \langle \Im G_t^{(N)}(x + \textrm{i}\eta )\rangle \bigg ) \widetilde{f}\bigg ( N \int _{\widetilde{E}_1}^{\widetilde{E}_2} \textrm{d}x \, \langle \Im G_t^{(N-k)}(x + \textrm{i}\eta )\rangle \bigg ) \nonumber \\ & \qquad - \textbf{E}f\bigg ( N \int _{E_1}^{E_2} \textrm{d}x \, \langle \Im G_s^{(N)}(x + \textrm{i}\eta )\rangle \bigg ) \widetilde{f}\bigg ( N \int _{\widetilde{E}_1}^{\widetilde{E}_2} \textrm{d}x \, \langle \Im G_s^{(N-k)}(x + \textrm{i}\eta )\rangle \bigg ) \bigg |\nonumber \\ & \quad \le C N^{-1/6 + C \varepsilon } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Ef(N∫E12dx⟨ℑGt(N)(x+iη)⟩)f~(N∫E~12dx⟨ℑGt(N-k)(x+iη)⟩)-Ef(N∫E12dx⟨ℑGs(N)(x+iη)⟩)f~(N∫E~12dx⟨ℑGs(N-k)(x+iη)⟩)|≤CN-1/6+Cεfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t,s \in [0, \infty ]$$\end{document}t,s∈[0,∞], for some constant C and large enough N.

Armed with Proposition 6.1, we can immediately prove Propositions 3.3 and 3.4. In the remainder of this section we will use Proposition 6.1 to identify the distribution of individual eigenvalues close to the edge of the spectrum. Translating (6.3) into a comparison of statistics of eigenvalues is a standard argument (see e.g. [39, Section 15.2]), and so omitted.

Proof of Proposition 3.3

Consider the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck flow (6.1) along which the first and second moments of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}_t^{(N)}$$\end{document}H^t(N) are preserved. Furthermore, we have6.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widehat{H}_t^{(N)}{\mathop {=}\limits ^{\textrm{d}}}e^{-t/2}\widehat{H}_0^{(N)}+\sqrt{1-e^{-t}}U, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H^t(N)=de-t/2H^0(N)+1-e-tU,for a GUE/GOE matrix U independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}_0^{(N)}$$\end{document}H^0(N). Next, we consider another flow (note that there is no drift term, unlike in (6.1))6.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}H_t^{(N)}=\frac{\textrm{d}B_t^{(N)}}{\sqrt{N}}, \qquad \quad H_0^{(N)}=e^{-t_1/2}H^{(N)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dHt(N)=dBt(N)N,H0(N)=e-t1/2H(N),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1=N^{-1/3+\omega _1}$$\end{document}t1=N-1/3+ω1. It is then easy to see that6.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_t^{(N)}{\mathop {=}\limits ^{\textrm{d}}}H_0^{(N)}+\sqrt{t}\widetilde{U}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ht(N)=dH0(N)+tU~,for a GUE/GOE matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{U}$$\end{document}U~ independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_0^{(N)}$$\end{document}H0(N).

The flows (6.1) and (6.5) induce a flow on the minors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_t^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}Ht(N-k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}_t^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}H^t(N-k) (see (3.2)–(3.3)). Using the shorthand notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1:=N$$\end{document}N1:=N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2:=N-k$$\end{document}N2:=N-k, we thus have6.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{ct_1}^{(N_l)}{\mathop {=}\limits ^{\textrm{d}}} \widehat{H}_{t_1}^{(N_l)}, \qquad \quad l=1,2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hct1(Nl)=dH^t1(Nl),l=1,2,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=c(t_1):=(1-e^{-t_1})/t_1$$\end{document}c=c(t1):=(1-e-t1)/t1.

We are now ready to conclude the proof. Denote the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}_t^{(N_l)}$$\end{document}H^t(Nl) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\lambda }_i^{(l)}(t)$$\end{document}λ^i(l)(t), for l=1,2l=1,2, and define the short–hand notations6.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda _i^{(l)}(t):= & (\lambda _i^{(l)}(t)-2(N_l/N_1)^{1/2})N_l^{2/3},\nonumber \\ \quad \widehat{\Lambda }_i^{(l)}(t):= & (\widehat{\lambda }_i^{(l)}(t)-2(N_l/N_1)^{1/2})N_l^{2/3}, \quad l=1,2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Λi(l)(t):=(λi(l)(t)-2(Nl/N1)1/2)Nl2/3,Λ^i(l)(t):=(λ^i(l)(t)-2(Nl/N1)1/2)Nl2/3,l=1,2,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(l)}(t)$$\end{document}λi(l)(t) being the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_t^{(N_l)}$$\end{document}Ht(Nl)

Then, using (6.3) and proceeding as in the proof of [39, Theorem 15.2], we find6.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \textbf{E}\left[ F\big (\Lambda _i^{(1)}(0)\big )G\big (\Lambda _j^{(2)}(0)\big )\right] =\textbf{E}\left[ F\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_i^{(1)}(t_1)\big )G\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_j^{(2)}(t_1)\big )\right] +\mathcal {O}\big (N^{-\delta }\big ), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EF(Λi(1)(0))G(Λj(2)(0))=EF(Λ^i(1)(t1))G(Λ^j(2)(t1))+O(N-δ),for some small fixed δ >0δ>0. Then, by (6.7), we have6.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}\left[ F\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_i^{(1)}(t_1)\big )G\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_j^{(2)}(t_1)\big )\right] =\textbf{E}\left[ F\big ({\Lambda }_i^{(1)}(ct_1)\big )G\big ({\Lambda }_j^{(2)}(ct_1)\big )\right] +\mathcal {O}\big (N^{-\delta }\big ).\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EF(Λ^i(1)(t1))G(Λ^j(2)(t1))=EF(Λi(1)(ct1))G(Λj(2)(ct1))+O(N-δ).Using that by assumption the independence (3.6) holds for the right-hand-side of (6.10), and using (6.7) again, we thus conclude6.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{E}\left[ F\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_i^{(1)}(t_1)\big )G\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_j^{(2)}(t_1)\big )\right] =\textbf{E}\left[ F\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_i^{(1)}(t_1)\big )\right] \textbf{E}\left[ G\big (\widehat{\Lambda }_j^{(2)}(t_1)\big )\right] +\mathcal {O}\big (N^{-\delta }\big ).\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EF(Λ^i(1)(t1))G(Λ^j(2)(t1))=EF(Λ^i(1)(t1))EG(Λ^j(2)(t1))+O(N-δ).This, together with another similar application of (6.9) (this time for the product of expectations), concludes the proof. □ □

Proof of Proposition 3.4

Given Proposition 6.1, the proof of Proposition 3.4 follows standard arguments; see, e.g., [39, Section 15.2]. □ □

It thus remains to give the proof of Proposition 6.1.

Proof of Proposition 6.1

First, we split the time interval [0, ∞ ][0,∞] in two parts, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_1 := [0,T]$$\end{document}I1:=[0,T] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_2 := [T, \infty ]$$\end{document}I2:=[T,∞], for T := 100 łog NT:=100logN. Then we distinguish four cases: For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t,s \in I_2$$\end{document}t,s∈I2, the claim is immediate by trivial perturbation theory, i.e. compare s,t ≥ Ts,t≥T directly with GOE/GUE. In fact, in this regime by (6.4) it follows that the distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{H}_t$$\end{document}H^t is entry-wise close to a GUE/GOE matrix: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert [e^{-t/2}\widehat{H}_0+\sqrt{1-e^{-t}}U]-U\Vert \lesssim N^{-10}$$\end{document}‖[e-t/2H^0+1-e-tU]-U‖≲N-10. This bound, together with Weyl’s inequality gives the desired result. The two cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in I_1$$\end{document}t∈I1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in I_2$$\end{document}s∈I2 (and vice versa) can be reduced to the fourth case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t,s \in I_1$$\end{document}t,s∈I1 via the same trivial perturbation argument.

We will now discuss the fourth, and last, case. For simplicity, we will henceforth assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x) = \widetilde{f}(x) = x$$\end{document}f(x)=f~(x)=x and prove Proposition 6.1 without integration. The adaptions to the more general situation are routine (see, e.g., [39, Proof of Theorem 17.4]) and so omitted. More precisely, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E, {\tilde{E}} \in \textbf{R}$$\end{document}E,E~∈R satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|E - 2| \le N^{-2/3 + \varepsilon }$$\end{document}|E-2|≤N-2/3+ε and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\widetilde{E} - 2\sqrt{1 - k/N}| \le N^{-2/3 + \varepsilon }$$\end{document}|E~-21-k/N|≤N-2/3+ε, we will show that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z = E + \textrm{i}\eta $$\end{document}z=E+iη and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{z} = \widetilde{E} + \textrm{i}\eta $$\end{document}z~=E~+iη, it holds that6.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (N \eta )^2 \bigg | \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\textbf{E}\langle \Im G_t^{(N)}(z)\rangle \, \langle \Im G_t^{(N-k)}(\tilde{z})\rangle \bigg | \lesssim N^{-1/6 + C \varepsilon } \,, \quad t \in [0,T] \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Nη)2|ddtE⟨ℑGt(N)(z)⟩⟨ℑGt(N-k)(z~)⟩|≲N-1/6+Cε,t∈[0,T].To do so, we will use that, for a smooth function f and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_t$$\end{document}Ht solving (6.1), by Itô’s formula, it holds that6.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t} \textbf{E}f(H_t)= & - Here, for a m-tuple of double indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\alpha }= (\alpha _1, ... , \alpha _m)$$\end{document}α=(α1,...,αm) we used the shorthand notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa (ab, (\alpha _1, ... , \alpha _m)) = \kappa (h_{ab}, h_{\alpha _1}, ... , h_{\alpha _m})$$\end{document}κ(ab,(α1,...,αm))=κ(hab,hα1,...,hαm) for the joint cumulant of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{ab}, h_{\alpha _1}, ... , h_{\alpha _m}$$\end{document}hab,hα1,...,hαm and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\boldsymbol{\alpha }} = \partial _{h_{\alpha _1}} ... \partial _{h_{\alpha _m}}$$\end{document}∂α=∂hα1...∂hαm and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{ab} = \partial _{h_{ab}}$$\end{document}∂ab=∂hab.

In order to simplify the following presentation, we will henceforth assume that there is no difference for off-diagonal (a ≠ ba≠b) and diagonal (a = ba=b) cumulants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa (ab, \boldsymbol{\alpha })$$\end{document}κ(ab,α) in (6.14). The general case can be handled with straightforward minor modifications and is thus left to the reader. Moreover, since t is fixed throughout the following argument, we will drop the subscript. For further ease of notation, we will write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G \equiv G_t^{(N)}(z)$$\end{document}G≡Gt(N)(z) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{G} \equiv G_t^{(N-k)}(\tilde{z})$$\end{document}G~≡Gt(N-k)(z~).

By Itô’s Lemma (6.13) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(H) = \langle \Im G \rangle \langle \Im \widetilde{G} \rangle $$\end{document}f(H)=⟨ℑG⟩⟨ℑG~⟩, cumulant expansion (6.14) (truncated at at some large but finite order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R \in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}R∈N), and using that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\kappa (ab, (\alpha _1,..., \alpha _m))| \lesssim N^{-(m+1)/2}$$\end{document}|κ(ab,(α1,...,αm))|≲N-(m+1)/2 in (6.15), we find6.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg | \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\textbf{E}\langle \Im G\rangle \, \langle \Im \widetilde{G}\rangle \bigg | \lesssim \sum _{m=3}^R N^{-m/2} \ \sum _{l=0}^m \bigg | \sum _{a,b} \textbf{E}\partial _{ab}^l \partial _{ba}^{m-l} \langle \Im G\rangle \, \langle \Im \widetilde{G}\rangle \bigg | + \mathcal {O}(N^{-100}) \,.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ddtE⟨ℑG⟩⟨ℑG~⟩|≲∑m=3RN-m/2∑l=0m|∑a,bE∂abl∂bam-l⟨ℑG⟩⟨ℑG~⟩|+O(N-100).Only the terms of order m=3m=3 in (6.15) have to be estimated carefully; the terms of order m ≥ 4m≥4 will be treated starting from (6.18). Now, for m=3m=3, we consider one exemplary term, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{ab}^l \partial _{ba}^{m-l}$$\end{document}∂abl∂bam-l hit only one of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Im G \rangle $$\end{document}⟨ℑG⟩ factors and leaves the other one intact, and another exemplary term, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{ab}^l \partial _{ba}^{m-l}$$\end{document}∂abl∂bam-l acts on both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Im G \rangle $$\end{document}⟨ℑG⟩ factors. As it is irrelevant for our estimates, we will henceforth ignore the difference between G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{G}$$\end{document}G~, as well as their adjoints \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^*$$\end{document}G∗ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{G}^*$$\end{document}G~∗, respectively, which arise by differentiation of ℑ GℑG. This is because, for the differentiated terms, we will neither exploit so-called Ward summations for different matrices G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{G}$$\end{document}G~, nor the smallness of ℑ GℑG compared to G. Moreover, the different normalization of the traces, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1}$$\end{document}N-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N-k)^{-1}$$\end{document}(N-k)-1, respectively, are irrelevant for our estimates.

Now, as the first exemplary term with m=3m=3 and l = 1l=1, we estimate (an additional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1}$$\end{document}N-1 factor stems from the normalized trace)6.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&N^{-5/2} \bigg | \textbf{E}\sum _{j, a,b} G_{ja} G_{bb} G_{aa} G_{bj} \langle \Im G \rangle \bigg | \\&\quad \lesssim \, N^{-5/2} \bigg | \sum _{j,a,b} \textbf{E}G_{ja} G_{bj} \langle \Im G \rangle \bigg | + N^{-5/2} N^{-1/3+ 2\varepsilon } \textbf{E}\sum _{j,a,b} |G_{ja} G_{bj}| \langle \Im G \rangle \\&\quad \lesssim \, N^{-5/2} N^{-1/3+ 2\varepsilon } \big | \textbf{E}\big ( G^2\big )_{\boldsymbol{1} \boldsymbol{1}} \big | + N^{-5/2} N^{-2/3+ 4\varepsilon } \textbf{E}\sum _{a,b} \sqrt{(GG^*)_{aa}} \sqrt{(GG^*)_{bb}} \\&\quad \lesssim \, N^{-1/2} N^{-1/3+ 5\varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2 + N^{-1/6+ 7 \varepsilon } /(N \eta )^2 \lesssim N^{-1/6 + 7 \varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2 \end{aligned} \nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N-5/2|E∑j,a,bGjaGbbGaaGbj⟨ℑG⟩|≲N-5/2|∑j,a,bEGjaGbj⟨ℑG⟩|+N-5/2N-1/3+2εE∑j,a,b|GjaGbj|⟨ℑG⟩≲N-5/2N-1/3+2ε|E(G2)11|+N-5/2N-2/3+4εE∑a,b(GG∗)aa(GG∗)bb≲N-1/2N-1/3+5ε/(Nη)2+N-1/6+7ε/(Nη)2≲N-1/6+7ε/(Nη)2where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{1} = (1,...,1) \in \textbf{C}^N$$\end{document}1=(1,...,1)∈CN. In the first step, we used the usual isotropic local law for Wigner matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{bb} = m_{\textrm{sc}} + \mathcal {O}_{\prec }(N^{-1/3+\varepsilon })$$\end{document}Gbb=msc+O≺(N-1/3+ε), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{\textrm{sc}} = m_{\textrm{sc}}(z)$$\end{document}msc=msc(z) denotes the Stieltjes transform of the semicircular density (see, e.g., [39, Theorem 6.7]). To go to the third line, we employed isotropic resummation in the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{ab} (G^2)_{ab} = (G^2)_{\boldsymbol{1} \boldsymbol{1}} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑ab(G2)ab=(G2)11,the averaged local law in the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Im G \rangle = \mathcal {O}_{\prec }(N^{-1/3+ \varepsilon })$$\end{document}⟨ℑG⟩=O≺(N-1/3+ε) (see, e.g., [39, Theorem 6.7]), where we additionally used that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Im m_{\textrm{sc}}| \lesssim N^{-1/3+\varepsilon }$$\end{document}|ℑmsc|≲N-1/3+ε at the edge regime, and a Schwarz inequality. In the penultimate step, we again used a Schwarz inequality, a Ward identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GG^* = \Im G/\eta $$\end{document}GG∗=ℑG/η, an isotropic law \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Im G)_{bb} = \mathcal {O}_{\prec }(N^{-1/3+\varepsilon })$$\end{document}(ℑG)bb=O≺(N-1/3+ε), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = N^{-2/3 - \varepsilon }$$\end{document}η=N-2/3-ε.

Next, as the second exemplary term with m=3m=3 and l =2l=2, we estimate (two additional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-1}$$\end{document}N-1 factors stem from two normalized traces)6.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&N^{-7/2} \bigg | \textbf{E}\sum _{i,j, a,b} G_{ia} G_{bb} G_{ai} G_{jb} G_{aj} \bigg | \\&\quad \lesssim \, N^{-7/2} \textbf{E}\sum _{i,j,a,b} |G_{ia} G_{ai} G_{jb} G_{aj}| \lesssim N^{-1/6 + 2 \varepsilon } /(N\eta )^2 \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N-7/2|E∑i,j,a,bGiaGbbGaiGjbGaj|≲N-7/2E∑i,j,a,b|GiaGaiGjbGaj|≲N-1/6+2ε/(Nη)2similarly to (6.16), my means of Schwarz inequalities (for the i and j summations; the a and b summations are estimated trivially by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^2$$\end{document}N2), Ward identities and the single resolvent laws from above.

Now we consider terms in (6.15) of order m ≥ 4m≥4. If only one \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Im G \rangle $$\end{document}⟨ℑG⟩ factor is hit by a derivative (cf. (6.16)), we estimate6.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&N^{-(m+2)/2} \textbf{E}\sum _{j,a,b} |G_{ja}...G_{bj} \langle \Im G \rangle | \lesssim N^{-(m+2)/2} \textbf{E}\sum _{j,a,b} |G_{ja}G_{bj} \langle \Im G \rangle | \\&\quad \lesssim \, N^{-(m-4)/2} N^{-1/3 + 5 \varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2 \le N^{-1/3 + 5 \varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2 \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N-(m+2)/2E∑j,a,b|Gja...Gbj⟨ℑG⟩|≲N-(m+2)/2E∑j,a,b|GjaGbj⟨ℑG⟩|≲N-(m-4)/2N-1/3+5ε/(Nη)2≤N-1/3+5ε/(Nη)2again by single resolvent laws and a Schwarz inequality together with a Ward identity. If both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Im G \rangle $$\end{document}⟨ℑG⟩ factors are hit by a derivative (cf. (6.17)), we estimate, similarly to (6.18),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} N^{-(m+4)/2} \textbf{E}\sum _{i,j,a,b} |G_{ia}...G_{bi} G_{ja} ... G_{bj}| \lesssim \,&N^{-(m+4)/2} \textbf{E}\sum _{i,j,a,b} |G_{ia}G_{bi} G_{ja} G_{bj}| \\ \lesssim \,&N^{-(m-4)/2} N^{-2/3 + 2 \varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2\\ \lesssim \,&N^{-2/3 + 2 \varepsilon }/(N \eta )^2 \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N-(m+4)/2E∑i,j,a,b|Gia...GbiGja...Gbj|≲N-(m+4)/2E∑i,j,a,b|GiaGbiGjaGbj|≲N-(m-4)/2N-2/3+2ε/(Nη)2≲N-2/3+2ε/(Nη)2.We have thus shown (6.12), which concludes the proof of Proposition 6.1. □ □

Subcritical regime: Proof of Theorem 2.4

In this section, we provide the proof of Theorem 2.4. We begin with the eigenvalue fluctuations.

Proposition 7.1

(Subcritical regime: Normal fluctuation on scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{k}/N$$\end{document}k/N) Fix a (small) constant ɛ >0ε>0. Let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}k∈N be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 1\ll k\le N^{2/3-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}1≪k≤N2/3-ε. Then for any fixed i, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)} - \lambda _i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λi(N)-λi(N-k) is asymptotically normal (see Footnote 3) with normalization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N/\sqrt{k}$$\end{document}N/k, i.e.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{N(\lambda _i^{(N)} - \lambda _i^{(N-k)}) - k}{\sqrt{k}} \sim \mathcal {N}(0,2/\beta ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(λi(N)-λi(N-k))-kk∼N(0,2/β)where β =1β=1 (real case) and β =2β=2 (complex case) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}(0,2/\beta )$$\end{document}N(0,2/β) is a real centered Gaussian random variable of variance 2/β 2/β.

The proof of Proposition 7.1 is given in Section 7.1. The following Proposition 7.2 concerns the eigenvector overlaps. Its proof is given in Section 7.2.

Proposition 7.2

(Subcritical regime: Eigenvector overlap) Fix a (small) constant ɛ >0ε>0. Let H be a Wigner matrix satisfying Assumption 2.1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \textbf{N}$$\end{document}k∈N be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ k\le N^{2/3-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}k≤N2/3-ε. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}_i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}wi(N-k) denote the i-th normalized eigenvector of its (N-k)(N-k)-minor. Then for any fixed i, any small (but fixed) constant δ >0δ>0, there exists a positive constant a>0a>0, such that, for N large enough,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{P}\Big (0\le 1-|\langle \boldsymbol{w}_i^{(N)}, {\boldsymbol{w}}_i^{(N-k)}\rangle |\le k N^{-\frac{2}{3}+\delta }\Big )\ge 1-N^{-a}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(0≤1-|⟨wi(N),wi(N-k)⟩|≤kN-23+δ)≥1-N-a.

Propositions 7.1 and 7.2 for i=1i=1 immediately prove parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 2.4, respectively. □ □

Proof of Proposition 7.1

The proof of Proposition 7.1 is split in several parts.

Preliminaries

We will, however, not need this precise information. We also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\xi _{\alpha }^{(n)}|\prec 1$$\end{document}|ξα(n)|≺1 using the moment bounds (2.1) on the matrix elements. As we are considering the subcritical regime, we will work with n∈ [N-k,N]n∈[N-k,N] only in this section.

We need the following results on rigidity and level repulsion of the eigenvalues. We refer to [37, Theorem 7.6] for rigidity (which, in a weaker form, has already been used in (4.10)), and [10, Proposition B.17] for the level repulsion, which can also be obtained by combining [17, Theorem 2.7] with [52, Remark 1.5].

Lemma 7.3

(Rigidity and level repulsion) For any n∈ [N-k,N]n∈[N-k,N], we have the following statements.

(i) [Rigidity] For any i∈ [n]i∈[n],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\lambda _i^{(n)}-\gamma _i^{(n)}| \prec \min \{i, n-i+1\}^{-1/3} n^{-2/3}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|λi(n)-γi(n)|≺min{i,n-i+1}-1/3n-2/3,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _i^{(n)}$$\end{document}γi(n) is defined in (4.7).

(ii) [Level repulsion] For any fixed i, and for any sufficiently small δ >0δ>0, there exists a small a>0a>0, such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{P}\big (|\lambda _i^{(n)}-\lambda _{i+1}^{(n)}|< N^{-2/3-\delta }\big )\le N^{-a}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(|λi(n)-λi+1(n)|<N-2/3-δ)≤N-a.

Finally, we denote the event of level repulsion at the i-th gap of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(n)}$$\end{document}H(n) as7.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_i^{(n)}(\delta )=\{|\lambda _i^{(n)}-\lambda _{i+1}^{(n)}|\ge N^{-2/3-\delta }\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ei(n)(δ)={|λi(n)-λi+1(n)|≥N-2/3-δ}.We will choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \equiv \delta (\varepsilon )$$\end{document}δ≡δ(ε) to be sufficiently small according to ɛ ε in the upper bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\le N^{2/3-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}k≤N2/3-ε.

We first consider the case i=1i=1, and later extend the discussion to generally fixed i (see Section 7.1.7).

Extension for i ≥ 2i≥2

Finally, we extend the above discussion to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λi(N)-λi(N-k) for any fixed i and get the same conclusion as (7.26). For instance, when i=2i=2, assuming the level repulsion for both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{(N)}-\lambda _2^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ1(N)-λ2(N) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _2^{(N)}-\lambda _3^{(N)}$$\end{document}λ2(N)-λ3(N), i.e, on event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_1^{(N)}(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2^{(N)}(\delta )$$\end{document}E1(N)(δ)∩E2(N)(δ), we can get from (7.13) that7.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N(\lambda _2^{(N)}-\lambda _2^{(N-1)})= |\xi _2^{(N)}|^2+O_\prec (N^{-1/3+C\delta }),\quad \text {on} \quad \mathcal {E}_1^{(N)}(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2^{(N)}(\delta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(λ2(N)-λ2(N-1))=|ξ2(N)|2+O≺(N-1/3+Cδ),onE1(N)(δ)∩E2(N)(δ),as a refinement of (7.14). The derivation of (7.27) can be done similarly to (7.11). Further, similarly to (7.14), one can derive7.28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N(\lambda _3^{(N)}-\lambda _3^{(N-1)})\ge |\xi _3^{(N)}|^2 \big (1+O_\prec (N^{-1/3+C\delta })\big ),\quad \text {on} \quad \mathcal {E}_1^{(N)}(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2^{(N)}(\delta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(λ3(N)-λ3(N-1))≥|ξ3(N)|2(1+O≺(N-1/3+Cδ)),onE1(N)(δ)∩E2(N)(δ).Combining (7.27) with (7.28) leads to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _2^{(N-1)}-\lambda _3^{(N-1)}&\ge \lambda _2^{(N)}-\lambda _3^{(N)}+\frac{1}{N}\Big (|\xi _3^{(N)}|^2-|\xi _2^{(N)}|^2\Big )\\&\quad +O_\prec (N^{-4/3+C\delta }), \quad \text {on} \quad \mathcal {E}_1^{(N)}(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2^{(N)}(\delta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ2(N-1)-λ3(N-1)≥λ2(N)-λ3(N)+1N(|ξ3(N)|2-|ξ2(N)|2)+O≺(N-4/3+Cδ),onE1(N)(δ)∩E2(N)(δ).Similarly to the proof of (7.16), we can iterate the above discussion and conclude\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{P}\Big ( \mathcal {E}_1(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2(\delta )\Big )\ge 1-N^{-a},\qquad \mathcal {E}_\alpha (\delta ):=\bigcap _{j=0}^k \mathcal {E}_{\alpha }^{(N-j)}(\delta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(E1(δ)∩E2(δ))≥1-N-a,Eα(δ):=⋂j=0kEα(N-j)(δ).by adjusting the values of δ δ and a slightly. On the event \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_1(\delta )\cap \mathcal {E}_2(\delta )$$\end{document}E1(δ)∩E2(δ), we have (7.27) if N is replaced by any N-jN-j with j∈ [k]j∈[k]. Summing up all these one step differences and applying the martingale CLT we can conclude the proof for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _2^{(N)}-\lambda _2^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λ2(N)-λ2(N-k). Further extension to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)}$$\end{document}λi(N)-λi(N-k) for any fixed i is analogous.

One can actually consider the joint distribution of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}(λi(N)-λi(N-k))’s with fixed i. It would be sufficient to study a generic linear combination of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\sum _{s=1}^k(|\xi _i^{(N-k+s)}|^2-1)]$$\end{document}[∑s=1k(|ξi(N-k+s)|2-1)]’s for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le C$$\end{document}1≤i≤C, according to (7.24) and its i-analogue. Such a linear combination is again a martingale adapted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {F}_{N,s}\}_{s=0}^{k}$$\end{document}{FN,s}s=0k. As an extension of (7.25), we need to compute the conditional variance for the martingale difference, which is now a linear combination of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(|\xi _i^{(N-k+s)}|^2-1)$$\end{document}(|ξi(N-k+s)|2-1)’s over i. By the orthogonality of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{w}^{(n-1)}_\alpha $$\end{document}wα(n-1)’s in (7.5), one can easily check that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(|\xi _i^{(N-k+s)}|^2-1)$$\end{document}(|ξi(N-k+s)|2-1)’s can be decoupled in the variance calculation of the martingale difference. This leads to the fact that the centered \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\lambda _i^{(N)}-\lambda _i^{(N-k)})$$\end{document}(λi(N)-λi(N-k))’s will converge to independent Gaussian variables. This concludes the proof of Proposition 7.1. □ □

Proof of Proposition 7.2

In this section, we turn to the eigenvector overlaps and prove Proposition 7.2. Just as in Section 7.1, we fix an index i which is independent of N.
